58. See note 45. Kierkegaard presumably refers here to the place where
Peder Madsens Gang [Alley[ entered Vimmdskaftet, part of the present-day
Stmget, a series of streets creating a pedestrian thoroughfare through the center
of the city. In Kierkegaard's day this alley was notorious for prostitution. See
also "Public Confession," Corsair Aj(air, p. 7, Kr,VXIll (SVXIll 401).
59. Poulsen delivered his address on the evening of June 24, 1844, in the
Ridehus of Christiansborg Palace. The speech resulted in legal charges by Orla
Lehmann, but Poulsen was acquitted. Sec Fcrdrelandet, 1922-24, June 25-28,
1845.
60. See Joel 2:28-29.
61. Poul Frcderik Barfod (1811-1896), politician, poet, and historian, notto
be confused with H. P. Barfod, editor of the Erst edition ofKierkegaard'sjour-
nals and papers, Ejicrladtc Papircr.
62. On June 25, 1845. the Scandinavian Association had a festival day in
Dyrehaven with a program including a song and two talks, one improvised, by
N.F.S. Grundtvig.
63. The flnal two paragraphs of this entry appear to be largely ironical and
fictive, including most likely the episode of the empty church. Although they
remained unpublished, these comments on Mynster are among the earliest inti-
mations ofKierkegaard's later criticism of this cherished friend of his father and
the pastor of his own youth.
64. Possibly a reference toW. H. Rothe (1777-1857), curate ofTrinity
Church in Copenhagen, 1822-30, and pastor, 1830-57.
65. Kierkegaard preached his probational sermon in this church on
March 24, 1844.
66. Kierkegaard did not publish this work. Part I of this projected work
appeared separately in Fcrdrela11det, June 24-27, 1848, under the title The Crisis
a11d a Crisis i11 the L!fe of an Actress (KWXVII). Portions of Part 2 were com-
pleted but remained unpublished. Sec Supplement, pp. 156-60 (Pap. VII' B
205-1 0). Part 3 on Christen Niemann Rosenkilde in the part of Hummer in
J. L. Heiberg's De Uadskill(~e, in Skuespil afj. L. Heibe~~. I-VII (Copenhagen:
1833-41; ASKB 1553-S<J), IV, pp. 223-348, was left unfinished (Pap. VIII2 B
172-74). Part 4 is Writi11g Sampler.


Notes to Pages 15 6-57
197
67. The pseudonym might literally be translated "The happy one from
St. Victor." The pseudonym was not used in a published work.
68. Kierkegaard developed this theme in a journal entry:
In Praise of Autumn
poetic .....
When the autumn comes with its brisk, invigorating coolness, when the
remnant of summer heat in the atmosphere is like a possibility, a motherly
solicitude lest the indulger be chilled, when one always has, so to speak, a light
coat at hand while the autumn winds increase-when autumn comes and the
transitoriness oflife elicits craving, when the forest does not stand secure as if it
would stand thus to all eternity but changes color even as one is looking at it,
because change inflames desire. When a woman remains secure and quiet, she
does not excite, but when she changes colors, the change signifies: hurry, hurry!
It is the same with autumn. In the summer the clouds never float as hurriedly as
in the autumn. And in the autumn the echo never thinks of pausing to relax in
the warm air of the woods-no, it rushes by without pausing.-JP Ill 2833
(Pap. VI A 89) n.d., 1845
Addition to Pap. VI A 89:
Everything that is present prompts criticism, but recollection disarms and
allows one to use ideality not to reject but to embellish the past.-JP III 2834
(Pap. VI A 90) n.d., 1845
In ma~~in of Pap. VI A 89:
Everything during autumn indeed reminds us of decline-and yet it strikes
me as being the most beautiful of seasons. When I begin to decline, would that
someone might think as well of me as I do ofautumn!-JP Ill 2835 (Pap. VI A
91) ll.d., 1845
69. See Supplement, p. 140 (Pap. VI B 226);JPV 5746 (Pap. VA 84).
70. The giant Ymer, slain by Bor's sons (Odin, Viii, and Ve), who then made
the universe out ofYmer's body. His flesh became the earth, his bones moun-
tains and stones, his hair trees and grass, his skull the heavens, his blood the seas
and rivers, his brains the clouds, and his marrow the dwarfs who live under the
earth and under stones. From his eyebrows the gods are said later to have made
a fortress, Midgaard, a defense against the giants.
Kierkegaard's library was richly stocked with works on mythology and folk-
lore, including N.F.S. Grundtvig, Nordens Mytoloy,ie eller Uds(~t over Eddala're11
(Copenhagen: 1808; ASKB 1948); Nordms Mythologi eller Sind-billedsprog
(Copenhagen: 1832; ASKB 1949); Thomas Crofton Croker, Irische Elfen-
miircheu, tr. Brothers Grimm (Leipzig: 1826; ASKB 1423); K. P. Moritz, Guder-
!Cl're (Copenhagen: 1847; ASKB 1946); Paul Friedrich A. Nitsch, Neues mytho-
logisches Wiirterbuch, I-II (Leipzig, Sorau: 1821; ASKB 1944-45); W. Vollmer,


19R
Notes to Pages 157-63
Vo//sta11d(Res Wiirterbuch dcr A1ytlwlogie allcr ,'\'atio11e11 (Stuttgart: 1836; ASKB
1942-43).
71. Christen Niemann Rosenkilde (1786-1861), a Danish actor famed
for comic roles. Rosenkilde played the part of Hummer in J. L. Heiberg's De
Uadskill(~c, Skuespil, IV, pp. 223-348. See also Postscript, Supplement, p. 130,
KWXll.2 (Pap. VII 1 B 88, p. 290).
72. On memory and recollection, see Stages, pp. 9-14, KWXl (SVVI 15-
20).
73. Perhaps a reference to J. L. Heiberg, De Uadskillige, Skuespi/, IV, p. 303.
74. Hans Lassen Martensen (1808-1884), professor of theology at the Uni-
versity of Copenhagen and successor to Jakob Peter Mynster as Bishop of
Sj;elland, returned to Denmark in the autumn of 1836 after a two-year period
of study and travel. In Berlin he studied with Henrich Steffens and Philipp
Marheineke and read Hegel's works. In Heidelberg he studied with Carl Daub.
He visited David Friedrich Strauss in Tiibingcn and Franz Baader in Munich,
where he also heard Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph v. Schelling lecture. See }I' II
1570; V 5200 (Pap. II A 52, 7); FragmCilts, pp. 0-7 and note 10, KWVII (SVIV
176-77).
75. See Fra.Rments, pp. 5-8, KWVII (SVIV 175-79).
76. See
Fra,~me11ts, Supplement, pp. 226-27, KW VII (Pap. X2 A 155,
p. 117).
77. A reference to Jakob Peter Mynster.
78. "Preface VI." See pp. 31-34.Jakob Peter Mynster had in an earlier refer-
ence to Fear and Trembli11g related it to an expression by the German philosopher
Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi (1743-1819): "]a, ich bill der Atheist tmd Gott-
lose, der ... liigCIJ will, wie Desdemona stcrh<'lld lo.~ (Yes, I am the atheist and
non-believer who ... wants to lie, as Desdemona dying liedj." See Jacobi a11
Fichte, Friedrich Heinrich jacobi's Werke, I-VI (Leipzig: 1812-25; ASKB 1722-
28) Ill, p. 37. For Mynster's comment, sec Kts, "Kirkelig Polemik," bttclli-
,Rfllsblade, ed. J. L. Heiberg, IV, 41-42, January 1, 1844, p. 105. "Kts" was
Mynster's pseudonym, formed from the initial consonant of the second syllable
of each name (Jakob Peter Mynster). See also Postscript, p. 262, KWXII.1 (SV
VII 221).
79. Mynster,J. L. Heiberg, and H. L. Martensen were parties to an extended
debate over the principle of contradiction. The exchange took place in a series
of articles in Tidsskriftfor Litteratur <~~ Kritik, 1839-45.
80. The pseudonymous editor of the ftrst volume of Either/Or. Sec KW IJI
(SVI).
81. The pseudonymous author of Fear a11d Trcmbli11g. See KWVI (SV IJI).
82. The pseudonymous author of Fragmellts and Postscript. See KWVII, XII
(SVIV, VII).
